Appendix A.4. GENIEv3
The GENIE neutrino interaction event generator [156,157]
is the primary generator used by most Fermilab neutrino-
beam experiments.
In this work we consider a variety
of model configurations in GENIE version 3.02.00 based
on publicly released GENIE tunes [116, 130, 492]. These
configurations alter the CCQE, 2p2h and FSI models, whilst
the modelling of other interaction modes remains the same.
CCQE interactions use a dipole axial form-factor with
MQE
A
= 0.99 GeV. RPP is simulated similarly to NEUT,
employing the Berger–Sehgal model and considering a
variety of resonances up to W = 1.7 GeV but neglecting
interferences between them. DIS and SIS cross sections are
described similarly to NEUT, but using a different custom
AGKY hadronisation model [142] for W ≤2.3 GeV, whilst
PYTHIA6 [141] is used for W > 3.0 GeV∗.
A linear
transition is considered between them.
10a, 10b, 10c:
GENIE configurations 10a and 10b differ
only in their treatment of FSI. GENIE 10b uses the hN
intranuclear cascade model, similar to NEUT and NuWro,
∗As of GENIE v3.4.0, PYTHIA8 is available as an alternative option,
and is expected to become the default in future versions of GENIE.
whilst 10a uses the hA empirical approach in which the
overall fate of hadrons in the cascade is decided in a
single interaction rather than in a stepped process [173].
GENIE 10c employs a new implementation of the INCL
intranuclear cascade model [183–186]. It should be noted
that this INCL implementation is expected to be updated in
future GENIE versions.
Like NEUT, the QE and 2p2h models used in
GENIEv3 10a, 10b and 10c are implementations of the
Valencia group’s calculations [168]. With respect to NEUT,
a more simplistic version of the nuclear ground state is used
in which the nuclear removal energy is fixed rather than
evolving with the initial state nucleon momentum.
CRPA:
The CRPA GENIE configuration uses the same
models as GENIE 10a except for CCQE and 2p2h. For
CCQE interactions it uses the GENIE implementation of
the CRPA model from the Ghent group [169, 170], as
detailed in Ref. [172]. Additionally, the version of CRPA
used in this work has a fast transition to the SuSAv2 CCQE
cross section model [197,201,211] at momentum transfers
between ∼500–700 MeV/c [197], as the latter uses an
unregularised nucleon–nucleon interaction within the RPA
calculation [59, 593], which may be better suited to low
energy and momentum transfer interactions. As in GENIE
10a, the hA FSI model is used to simulate the propagation
of final-state hadrons through the nucleus. The CRPA QE
model includes the role of FSI via a distorted wavefunction
approach on the outgoing lepton kinematics and so FSI is
considered using two separate approaches.
The GENIE CRPA model uses the SuSAv2 2p2h
model, as described in Ref. [197], which makes various
different
theory
assumptions
to
the
Valencia
model
resulting in a significantly different 2p2h prediction. The
pair contributions in SuSAv2 2p2h are approximately 80%
proton–neutron and, like the Valencia model case, also
dependent on the interaction kinematics.
Appendix B. Differences in electron and muon
neutrino–nucleon RPP interactions
When considering a fixed incoming neutrino energy,
lepton mass differences imply that electron and muon
(anti)neutrinos have different allowed ranges of energy and
momentum, thereby changing the cross section.
Whilst
such effects have been studied in some detail for CCQE
interactions [262–265], less literature is available for
other channels, despite this potentially being an important
effect, especially when the energy is near the process
threshold. This can be particularly important for resonant
pion interactions where, for a given fixed neutrino energy,
the differences in kinematically allowed interaction phase
space renders electron and muon (anti)neutrinos sensitive
to different resonant (and non-resonant) structures. This
leads to large differences in the ratio of their cross sections


## Page 85


CP-violation or Nuclear Excitation?
85
near the threshold energy of the prominent resonances (for
example, ∆(1232)).
This is demonstrated in Tab. B1,
which shows the ratios of the total neutrino–nucleon cross
section for electron and muon (anti)neutrinos in RPP
interactions considering for four different models of single
pion production.
The four models shown in Tab. B1 are: the Rein–
Sehgal model [91], which has been implemented with
variations in both GENIE and NEUT; the MK model
which refers to the model by Monireh Kabirnezhad [64];
the DCC model which refers to the dynamically coupled
channels model by Nakamura et al [87]; and the HNV
model which refers to the Hernandez, Nieves, and Vicente-
Vacas model [93] (occasionally called the Valencia single
pion model).
The Rein–Sehgal model is the simplest, and attempts
to describe a wide range of resonances up to a hadronic
mass of 2.0 GeV/c2 (15–20 resonances in total) using the
quark-harmonic oscillator [594], which has been subject to
a variety of criticisms, including violation of unitarity, but
continues to be in use by experiments [90, 595].
It has
a simplistic recipe for the non-resonant background, and
includes resonance–resonance interference, but not inter-
ference between the non-resonant and resonant contribu-
tions. The HNV model simplifies the structure of the res-
onances considerably, focussing on the dominant ∆(1232)
and D13(1520) resonances and a non-resonant background
based on a non-linear sigma model which is fixed from
chiral symmetries. It includes their interferences and par-
tially restores unitarity. The MK model uses ingredients
similar to the HNV model, such as aspects of the non-
resonant background and the method to restore unitarity,
and attempts to describe a broader range of hadronic masses
(similar to the Rein–Sehgal model) by using a meson-
dominance model that respects quantum chromodynamics.
The DCC model similarly targets a wide range of reso-
nances, and uniquely accounts for that resonance interac-
tions can happen through a number of channels which are
strongly coupled (for instance πN, ππN, ηN, KΛ, KΣ).
All of these couplings need to be modelled if unitarity is to
be properly conserved, which is what the DCC model ad-
dresses. Importantly, the HNV, MK and DCC models have
been extensively benchmarked against electron-scattering
data, and the latter two have additionally been benchmarked
against pion and photon induced pion production data. Gen-
erally speaking, all the models are expected to see similar
overall behaviour, for instance ∆(1232) dominance, but the
details of that dominance, such as the turn-on of the interac-
tion in hadronic mass which the threshold region is sensitive
to, differ considerably.
Tab. B1 shows that at 0.63 GeV of neutrino energy,
the neutrino–nucleon cross section for electron neutrinos is
23–29% larger than muon neutrinos depending on model,
which decreases to 2–3% at 2 GeV, and 1% at 5 GeV.
Thus, experiments at higher energy are less sensitive to
the threshold behaviour for the resonant processes, but are
instead more sensitive to the threshold behaviour of higher
energy processes, such as DIS. This is a relatively little-
studied effect that could impact current and future neutrino
oscillation experiments.
